Setting: K-armed & contextual. Arms (K) = 10. Horizon (T steps) = 1,000. 30 runs, seed 7.
Ranked by final regret (lower is better).

Each arm pays 1 with probability μ ~ U(0, 1), else 0. Gaps are usually large.

### Linear contextual

Each round shows a context x = (1, z₁…z₄), z ~ N(0, 1). Arm a pays N(θₐ·x, 1) with a hidden θₐ ~ N(0, 0.5²I). Without the context, only the average arm can be learned.

### Adversarial, switching trap

An oblivious adversary in three phases of doubling length (T/7, 2T/7, 4T/7). Arm B leads while every arm pays well; then arm A takes over while every arm pays poorly; then all pay well again and A stays 0.2 ahead. Learners that trust averages of old rewards cling to B, then starve A because its average is dragged down by the lean phase.

### Stochastically constrained adversary

Zimmert & Seldin's construction: the gaps are fixed (best arm always ahead by Δₐ ~ U(0.1, 0.2)) but all means jump together between a low level (0.25) and a high level (0.9) in phases that grow by 1.6×. Best-of-both-worlds methods keep logarithmic regret; stale averages fool UCB and Thompson sampling.

### Stochastic with adversarial corruptions

Bernoulli arms with μ ~ U(0, 1), but an adversary corrupts the first C = ⌈4√T⌉ rounds: the best arm pays 0 and the worst arm pays 1. Regret is still measured against the true means.

### Linear bandit, shared θ

Every round each arm gets fresh features xₜ,ₐ ~ N(0, I₈). Arm a pays θ·xₜ,ₐ + N(0, 1) with one hidden θ on the unit sphere, shared by all arms. Ignoring the features, every arm is equally good on average.

### Linear bandit, fixed arm set

K fixed arms xₐ drawn uniformly on the unit sphere in R⁵ (the same features every round). Arm a pays θ·xₐ + N(0, 0.5²) with a hidden unit θ. A finite-armed linear bandit, the home ground of G-optimal design and elimination.

### Logistic (GLM) bandit

Fresh features xₜ,ₐ ~ N(0, I₆) every round; arm a pays 1 with probability σ(θ·xₜ,ₐ), σ the logistic function, with a hidden θ of norm 2. A click-through model where rewards saturate near 0 and 1.

### Sparse high-dimensional linear

Fresh features in R⁴⁰ every round, xₜ,ₐ = √½·uₜ + √½·eₜ,ₐ with u, e ~ N(0, I) (a shared per-round part makes arms correlated); reward θ·xₜ,ₐ + N(0, 1). Only s = 4 of the 40 coordinates of θ are non-zero (‖θ‖ = 1). Methods that assume sparsity (Lasso) need far fewer samples than d-dimensional ridge regression.

### Bilinear, low-rank

Each round a user context xₜ ~ N(0, I₅); arm a is an item with fixed features zₐ on the unit sphere in R⁵. Reward xₜᵀΘzₐ + N(0, 0.5²) with a hidden rank-2 Θ (singular values 1.5 and 1). Layout: row a of X is vec(xₜzₐᵀ), X[a·25 + 5i + j] = xₜ[i]·zₐ[j], so the problem is linear in 25 dimensions but only 2·(5+5)−4 = 16 directions matter.

### Smooth on a graph

Arms are the nodes of a random graph (a ring plus random chords). Means are smooth on the graph: μ = Σₖ αₖuₖ over the 8 smoothest Laplacian eigenvectors uₖ with αₖ ~ N(0, 1)/(0.2 + λₖ), then centred and scaled to standard deviation 1; rewards add N(0, 1). Row a of X holds node a's coordinates in the 8 smoothest eigenvectors (zero-padded when K < 8); info.spectral carries the eigenvalues and eigenvectors.

### Linear bandit, switching θ

Like the shared-θ linear bandit (fresh features xₜ,ₐ ~ N(0, I₈), reward θ·xₜ,ₐ + N(0, 1)), but the hidden unit θ is redrawn at four evenly spaced change points.

### Rotting arms (rested)

Arms wear out with use: arm a pays N(μₐ, 0.1²), μₐ ~ U(0.3, 1), until it has been pulled mₐ times, then its mean drops by dₐ ~ U(0.3, 0.8) for good. The wear-out points mₐ are log-uniform around T/(2K) pulls. Pulling whichever arm is best right now (the greedy oracle) is optimal; an average over all past pulls keeps overrating a worn-out arm.

### Rising arms (rested)

Arms improve with use, like models that learn from training: the n-th pull of arm a has mean cₐ·(1 − e^(−(n+1)/τₐ)), rewards N(mean, 0.1²). One late bloomer has a high ceiling (c ~ U(0.85, 1)) but learns slowly (τ ~ U(T/40, T/8)); the others learn fast (τ log-uniform in [T/500, T/50]) to modest ceilings (c ~ U(0.3, 0.7)). Committing to the late bloomer is optimal, but early on it looks like the worst arm.

### Bernoulli, local changes

Piecewise-stationary Bernoulli arms, μ ~ U(0.1, 0.9) at the start. At four evenly spaced change points only one arm moves: the current best arm breaks and drops to μ ~ U(0.05, 0.25). The other arms never change, so a per-arm change detector that restarts only the broken arm keeps everything else it has learned.

### Non-linear contextual

Each round shows a shared context x ~ U(−1, 1)⁵. Arm a pays N(cos(3θₐ·x) + βₐ·x, 0.5²) with θₐ a random unit vector and βₐ ~ N(0, 0.3²I). A linear model sees only the small linear part, so the best arm per context needs a non-linear learner.

### Non-linear contextual, binary

Same hidden functions as the non-linear environment, but arm a pays 1 with probability σ(2.5·f_a(x)), a logistic link on f_a(x) = cos(3θₐ·x) + βₐ·x.

### Heavy-tailed (Student-t, ν = 2.2)

Means μ ~ N(0, 1); rewards are μ plus Student-t noise with ν = 2.2 (scale 1, variance ≈ 11, third moment infinite). Rare huge outliers wreck sample means.

### Risky best arm (skewed losses)

Means μ ~ U(0, 1); rewards are μ + σₐ(1 − E), E ~ Exp(1), a long left tail. Other arms have σₐ ~ U(0.2, 1), but the best-mean arm has σ = 2.5, so its CVaR and mean–variance score are poor.

### Bernoulli with side observations

Bernoulli arms, μ ~ U(0, 1), plus a random undirected feedback graph (Erdős–Rényi, p = 0.25, self-loops): pulling an arm also reveals its neighbours' rewards to algorithms that listen.
